I'm Dr. Smith. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi Dr. Smith. Well, what brought me in is that I'm having some trouble at work. I just keep leaving my station on the floor, or I'll pop up out of my chair during meetings when I'm really supposed to stay put. My supervisor says it's becoming a safety problem, because I just take off before I even think about it.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the trouble staying in your seat, do you also find that you frequently have difficulty focusing on tasks or completing projects at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like when I'm doing the inventory logs, or... or trying to read the CNC training pages. I start out really trying, but after just a few minutes, the numbers or the words just kind of blur together. I have to keep going back and rereading because I realize I didn't actually take any of it in.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your time in school, did you struggle with these same issues of staying in your seat or focusing on your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it was pretty much exactly like that back then, too. Even in elementary school, I was always the kid who just couldn't stay in my seat. I talked over the teacher a lot, and I was always getting notes sent home because I'd blurt things out or just start wandering around the room when I wasn't supposed to. So, yeah, it's been an issue for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your struggles with focus and your struggles with sitting still are equally problematic for you, or is one of those two issues significantly more disruptive to your life than the other?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if one is worse than the other. Um... they both cause a lot of problems for me. I guess the not sitting still is what gets me yelled at the most by my supervisor, just because of the safety rules on the floor. But the focus stuff makes it so I can't actually do my job right. So... yeah, they're probably about equal. I haven't really thought about which one is more disruptive.\n\nDoctor: When you are in those meetings or trying to complete your work, do you find that your mind feels like it's racing with thoughts, or is it more that your mind goes completely blank and you just can't get it to focus on the task?\n\nPatient: Um, it's definitely not blank. It's more like... my mind is just going a million miles an hour. Like, I'll be trying to read a work order, and suddenly I'm thinking about what I need to get at the grocery store, and then I'm wondering if I left my car unlocked, and then I'm thinking about something my friend said yesterday. It just never really stops or slows down enough for me to just look at what's right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your work and your school days, do these struggles with focus and staying still cause you significant difficulty in your home life or your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's mostly a problem at work. Like I said, it's really putting my job on the line. Um... I'm supposed to be working toward some certifications, but that's at risk right now. My supervisors have actually written me up a few times because of safety issues and just... not meeting production goals. And it affects how I get along with people there, too. My coworkers are getting frustrated, and they're a lot less willing to partner with me when we have to do handoffs on the floor. So, yeah, it's definitely causing some major issues there.\n\nDoctor: Looking at your personal life outside of work, do you find that you also struggle with being disorganized, losing things, or being forgetful in your daily routine at home?\n\nPatient: I mean, I forget normal stuff all the time. It happens at home, but it's just so much worse at work. Like, I'll forget to clock a step on the checklist, or I'll completely forget to answer a supervisor's text message. Or I'll get all the way out to the floor and realize I forgot to bring my safety glasses from my locker. It just happens constantly, and then it makes it look like I just don't care, but I literally just forget.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the trouble you have at work, do you find that these same issues with being disorganized, forgetful, or unable to sit still frequently cause problems for you in your social life or at home with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Um, not really? I mean, I guess I'm still the same way at home, but it doesn't really cause big problems with my family or my friends. Like, my friends don't really care if I'm moving around a lot or if I forget something. They're pretty used to it, I guess. And at home, I just kind of do my own thing, so nobody's really getting on my case about losing stuff or not sitting still. It's pretty much just at work where it's actually messing things up for me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that these issues don't cause major problems with your friends or family, but have you ever felt that your difficulty staying still or focusing has negatively impacted your ability to maintain friendships or caused conflict within your household?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, I haven't noticed anything like that. Um... I don't really have trouble keeping friends, and we don't get into arguments at home over me moving around or whatever. Like I was saying, it really is just my job where it's causing actual trouble for me.\n\nDoctor: Even if your friends and family aren't upset by your behavior, do you find that your forgetfulness, difficulty staying still, or trouble focusing ever actually prevent you from successfully managing your personal responsibilities, such as paying bills, keeping appointments, or maintaining a clean living space?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't think so? I pay my rent on time and everything. My place isn't, like, perfectly spotless, but it's not a disaster either. I just do what I need to do. Like I said, the only place I'm really dropping the ball and getting in trouble for it is at the plant.\n\nDoctor: When you are at home or in situations where you don't have to be productive, do you still feel an internal sense of restlessness or a constant need to be doing something?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um... it's really obvious at the plant. Like, when I try to stand still by my machine, or if I have to sit through a training session, it feels like there's this... pressure building up inside my body. I just get this intense feeling where I have to move, or pace around, or stretch, or just go do something right then. It's like I just can't stay put.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I have to keep going back and rereading because I realize I didn't actually take any of it in.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your time in school, did you struggle with these same issues of staying in your seat or focusing on your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it was pretty much exactly like that back then, too. Even in elementary school, I was always the kid who just couldn't stay in my seat. I talked over the teacher a lot, and I was always getting notes sent home because I'd blurt things out or just start wandering around the room when I wasn't supposed to. So, yeah, it's been an issue for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your struggles with focus and your struggles with sitting still are equally problematic for you, or is one of those two issues significantly more disruptive to your life than the other?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if one is worse than the other. But the focus stuff makes it so I can't actually do my job right. So... yeah, they're probably about equal. I haven't really thought about which one is more disruptive.\n\nDoctor: When you are in those meetings or trying to complete your work, do you find that your mind feels like it's racing with thoughts, or is it more that your mind goes completely blank and you just can't get it to focus on the task?\n\nPatient: Um, it's definitely not blank. It's more like... my mind is just going a million miles an hour. Like, I'll be trying to read a work order, and suddenly I'm thinking about what I need to get at the grocery store, and then I'm wondering if I left my car unlocked, and then I'm thinking about something my friend said yesterday. It just never really stops or slows down enough for me to just look at what's right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your work and your school days, do these struggles with focus and staying still cause you significant difficulty in your home life or your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's mostly a problem at work. Like I said, it's really putting my job on the line. Um... I'm supposed to be working toward some certifications, but that's at risk right now. My supervisors have actually written me up a few times because of safety issues and just... not meeting production goals. And it affects how I get along with people there, too. My coworkers are getting frustrated, and they're a lot less willing to partner with me when we have to do handoffs on the floor. So, yeah, it's definitely causing some major issues there.\n\nDoctor: Looking at your personal life outside of work, do you find that you also struggle with being disorganized, losing things, or being forgetful in your daily routine at home?\n\nPatient: I mean, I forget normal stuff all the time. It happens at home, but it's just so much worse at work. Like, I'll forget to clock a step on the checklist, or I'll completely forget to answer a supervisor's text message. It just happens constantly, and then it makes it look like I just don't care, but I literally just forget.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the trouble you have at work, do you find that these same issues with being disorganized, forgetful, or unable to sit still frequently cause problems for you in your social life or at home with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Um, not really? I pay my rent on time and everything. My place isn't, like, perfectly spotless, but it's not a disaster either. I just do what I need to do. Like I said, the only place I'm really dropping the ball and getting in trouble for it is at the plant.\n\nDoctor: When you are at home or in situations where you don't have to be productive, do you still feel an internal sense of restlessness or a constant need to be doing something?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's like I just can't stay put.\n\nDoctor: When you are at home, do you ever find yourself struggling to complete tasks or stay on track with your personal chores because you get easily distracted or lose your focus?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I'm Dr. Smith. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi Dr. Smith. Well, what brought me in is that I'm having some trouble at work. I just keep leaving my station on the floor, or I'll pop up out of my chair during meetings when I'm really supposed to stay put. My supervisor says it's becoming a safety problem, because I just take off before I even think about it.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the trouble staying in your seat, do you also find that you frequently have difficulty focusing on tasks or completing projects at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like when I'm doing the inventory logs, or... or trying to read the CNC training pages. I start out really trying, but after just a few minutes, the numbers or the words just kind of blur together. I have to keep going back and rereading because I realize I didn't actually take any of it in.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your time in school, did you struggle with these same issues of staying in your seat or focusing on your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it was pretty much exactly like that back then, too. Even in elementary school, I was always the kid who just couldn't stay in my seat. I talked over the teacher a lot, and I was always getting notes sent home because I'd blurt things out or just start wandering around the room when I wasn't supposed to. So, yeah, it's been an issue for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your struggles with focus and your struggles with sitting still are equally problematic for you, or is one of those two issues significantly more disruptive to your life than the other?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if one is worse than the other. Um... they both cause a lot of problems for me. I guess the not sitting still is what gets me yelled at the most by my supervisor, just because of the safety rules on the floor. But the focus stuff makes it so I can't actually do my job right. So... yeah, they're probably about equal. I haven't really thought about which one is more disruptive.\n\nDoctor: When you are in those meetings or trying to complete your work, do you find that your mind feels like it's racing with thoughts, or is it more that your mind goes completely blank and you just can't get it to focus on the task?\n\nPatient: Um, it's definitely not blank. It's more like... my mind is just going a million miles an hour. Like, I'll be trying to read a work order, and suddenly I'm thinking about what I need to get at the grocery store, and then I'm wondering if I left my car unlocked, and then I'm thinking about something my friend said yesterday. It just never really stops or slows down enough for me to just look at what's right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your work and your school days, do these struggles with focus and staying still cause you significant difficulty in your home life or your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's mostly a problem at work. Like I said, it's really putting my job on the line. Um... I'm supposed to be working toward some certifications, but that's at risk right now. My supervisors have actually written me up a few times because of safety issues and just... not meeting production goals. And it affects how I get along with people there, too. My coworkers are getting frustrated, and they're a lot less willing to partner with me when we have to do handoffs on the floor. So, yeah, it's definitely causing some major issues there.\n\nDoctor: Looking at your personal life outside of work, do you find that you also struggle with being disorganized, losing things, or being forgetful in your daily routine at home?\n\nPatient: I mean, I forget normal stuff all the time. It happens at home, but it's just so much worse at work. Like, I'll forget to clock a step on the checklist, or I'll completely forget to answer a supervisor's text message. Or I'll get all the way out to the floor and realize I forgot to bring my safety glasses from my locker. It just happens constantly, and then it makes it look like I just don't care, but I literally just forget.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the trouble you have at work, do you find that these same issues with being disorganized, forgetful, or unable to sit still frequently cause problems for you in your social life or at home with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Um, not really? I mean, I guess I'm still the same way at home, but it doesn't really cause big problems with my family or my friends. Like, my friends don't really care if I'm moving around a lot or if I forget something. They're pretty used to it, I guess. And at home, I just kind of do my own thing, so nobody's really getting on my case about losing stuff or not sitting still. It's pretty much just at work where it's actually messing things up for me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that these issues don't cause major problems with your friends or family, but have you ever felt that your difficulty staying still or focusing has negatively impacted your ability to maintain friendships or caused conflict within your household?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, I haven't noticed anything like that. Um... I don't really have trouble keeping friends, and we don't get into arguments at home over me moving around or whatever. Like I was saying, it really is just my job where it's causing actual trouble for me.\n\nDoctor: Even if your friends and family aren't upset by your behavior, do you find that your forgetfulness, difficulty staying still, or trouble focusing ever actually prevent you from successfully managing your personal responsibilities, such as paying bills, keeping appointments, or maintaining a clean living space?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't think so? I pay my rent on time and everything. My place isn't, like, perfectly spotless, but it's not a disaster either. I just do what I need to do. Like I said, the only place I'm really dropping the ball and getting in trouble for it is at the plant.\n\nDoctor: When you are at home or in situations where you don't have to be productive, do you still feel an internal sense of restlessness or a constant need to be doing something?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um... it's really obvious at the plant. I just get this intense feeling where I have to move, or pace around, or stretch, or just go do something right then. It's like I just can't stay put.\n\nDoctor: When you are at home, do you ever find yourself struggling to complete tasks or stay on track with your personal chores because you get easily distracted or lose your focus?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just work where I'm having all these problems.\n\nDoctor: Even though you mentioned you don't get in trouble at home, do you find that you have to put in significantly more effort or energy than other people to keep track of your personal tasks and stay on top of your responsibilities at home?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I'm Dr. Smith. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi Dr. Smith. Well, what brought me in is that I'm having some trouble at work. I just keep leaving my station on the floor, or I'll pop up out of my chair during meetings when I'm really supposed to stay put. My supervisor says it's becoming a safety problem, because I just take off before I even think about it.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the trouble staying in your seat, do you also find that you frequently have difficulty focusing on tasks or completing projects at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like when I'm doing the inventory logs, or... or trying to read the CNC training pages. I start out really trying, but after just a few minutes, the numbers or the words just kind of blur together. I have to keep going back and rereading because I realize I didn't actually take any of it in.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your time in school, did you struggle with these same issues of staying in your seat or focusing on your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it was pretty much exactly like that back then, too. Even in elementary school, I was always the kid who just couldn't stay in my seat. I talked over the teacher a lot, and I was always getting notes sent home because I'd blurt things out or just start wandering around the room when I wasn't supposed to. So, yeah, it's been an issue for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your struggles with focus and your struggles with sitting still are equally problematic for you, or is one of those two issues significantly more disruptive to your life than the other?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if one is worse than the other. Um... they both cause a lot of problems for me. I guess the not sitting still is what gets me yelled at the most by my supervisor, just because of the safety rules on the floor. But the focus stuff makes it so I can't actually do my job right. So... yeah, they're probably about equal. I haven't really thought about which one is more disruptive.\n\nDoctor: When you are in those meetings or trying to complete your work, do you find that your mind feels like it's racing with thoughts, or is it more that your mind goes completely blank and you just can't get it to focus on the task?\n\nPatient: Um, it's definitely not blank. It's more like... my mind is just going a million miles an hour. Like, I'll be trying to read a work order, and suddenly I'm thinking about what I need to get at the grocery store, and then I'm wondering if I left my car unlocked, and then I'm thinking about something my friend said yesterday. It just never really stops or slows down enough for me to just look at what's right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your work and your school days, do these struggles with focus and staying still cause you significant difficulty in your home life or your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's mostly a problem at work. Like I said, it's really putting my job on the line. Um... I'm supposed to be working toward some certifications, but that's at risk right now. My supervisors have actually written me up a few times because of safety issues and just... not meeting production goals. And it affects how I get along with people there, too. My coworkers are getting frustrated, and they're a lot less willing to partner with me when we have to do handoffs on the floor. So, yeah, it's definitely causing some major issues there.\n\nDoctor: Looking at your personal life outside of work, do you find that you also struggle with being disorganized, losing things, or being forgetful in your daily routine at home?\n\nPatient: I mean, I forget normal stuff all the time. It happens at home, but it's just so much worse at work. Like, I'll forget to clock a step on the checklist, or I'll completely forget to answer a supervisor's text message. Or I'll get all the way out to the floor and realize I forgot to bring my safety glasses from my locker. It just happens constantly, and then it makes it look like I just don't care, but I literally just forget.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the trouble you have at work, do you find that these same issues with being disorganized, forgetful, or unable to sit still frequently cause problems for you in your social life or at home with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Um, not really? I mean, I guess I'm still the same way at home, but it doesn't really cause big problems with my family or my friends. Like, my friends don't really care if I'm moving around a lot or if I forget something. They're pretty used to it, I guess. And at home, I just kind of do my own thing, so nobody's really getting on my case about losing stuff or not sitting still. It's pretty much just at work where it's actually messing things up for me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that these issues don't cause major problems with your friends or family, but have you ever felt that your difficulty staying still or focusing has negatively impacted your ability to maintain friendships or caused conflict within your household?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, I haven't noticed anything like that. Um... I don't really have trouble keeping friends, and we don't get into arguments at home over me moving around or whatever. Like I was saying, it really is just my job where it's causing actual trouble for me.\n\nDoctor: Even if your friends and family aren't upset by your behavior, do you find that your forgetfulness, difficulty staying still, or trouble focusing ever actually prevent you from successfully managing your personal responsibilities, such as paying bills, keeping appointments, or maintaining a clean living space?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't think so? I pay my rent on time and everything. My place isn't, like, perfectly spotless, but it's not a disaster either. I just do what I need to do. Like I said, the only place I'm really dropping the ball and getting in trouble for it is at the plant.\n\nDoctor: When you are at home or in situations where you don't have to be productive, do you still feel an internal sense of restlessness or a constant need to be doing something?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um... it's really obvious at the plant. Like, when I try to stand still by my machine, or if I have to sit through a training session, it feels like there's this... pressure building up inside my body. I just get this intense feeling where I have to move, or pace around, or stretch, or just go do something right then. It's like I just can't stay put.\n\nDoctor: When you are at home, do you ever find yourself struggling to complete tasks or stay on track with your personal chores because you get easily distracted or lose your focus?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I get my chores done fine. It's not like I'm leaving things half-finished all over the house or forgetting what I was doing. When I'm home, I just do whatever needs doing and it's fine. It's just work where I'm having all these problems.\n\nDoctor: Even though you mentioned you don't get in trouble at home, do you find that you have to put in significantly more effort or energy than other people to keep track of your personal tasks and stay on top of your responsibilities at home?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, it doesn't feel like I'm trying extra hard or anything. I just... I know what I have to do around the house and I do it. It doesn't take up a bunch of energy to remember to take out the trash or whatever. It's really just a normal amount of effort, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you are in those moments at home where you say you are getting your chores done or relaxing, do you ever find that you are actually moving around, fidgeting, or needing to change activities more often than other people you know, even if it doesn't cause you any trouble?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Um... I don't think I move around more than anyone else when I'm at home. Once I'm done with whatever I need to do, I just sit down and watch TV or hang out. I don't feel like I'm always fidgeting or jumping from one thing to another there. It really is just at the plant where I get that restless feeling I was talking about.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your childhood and adolescence, were there any settings other than school—such as at home, during extracurricular activities, or while visiting relatives—where you were frequently told that you couldn't sit still or that you weren't paying attention?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly it was just at school. Even back in elementary school, I was always the kid who couldn't stay in my seat. I'd talk over the teacher all the time, and I got a lot of notes sent home because I kept blurting things out or just wandering around the room when I wasn't supposed to. But outside of school, like at home or with family, I don't really remember anyone making a big deal about it. It was pretty much just the teachers getting on my case.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the school environment you mentioned, were there any other settings as a child or teenager, such as at home or during social activities, where you were frequently told that you couldn't sit still or that you weren't paying attention?\n\nPatient: No, like I just said, it was really just at school. I don't remember anyone at home or anywhere else telling me I was hyper or anything. It was just my teachers who were always getting onto me.\n\nDoctor: Since you mentioned your struggles were primarily at school as a child and are primarily at work now, can you think of any other specific settings, like extracurricular clubs, sports teams, or part-time jobs you might have had as a teenager, where these behaviors caused similar problems?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I didn't really do any clubs or sports or anything like that. I did have a couple of fast-food jobs before I got hired at the plant, but you're always on your feet and moving fast in those places anyway, so nobody ever complained about me being restless or anything. So, yeah, it really was just school.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Um... they both cause a lot of problems for me. I guess the not sitting still is what gets me yelled at the most by my supervisor, just because of the safety rules on the floor. But the focus stuff makes it so I can't actually do my job right. So... yeah, they're probably about equal. I haven't really thought about which one is more disruptive.\n\nDoctor: When you are in those meetings or trying to complete your work, do you find that your mind feels like it's racing with thoughts, or is it more that your mind goes completely blank and you just can't get it to focus on the task?\n\nPatient: Um, it's definitely not blank. It's more like... my mind is just going a million miles an hour. Like, I'll be trying to read a work order, and suddenly I'm thinking about what I need to get at the grocery store, and then I'm wondering if I left my car unlocked, and then I'm thinking about something my friend said yesterday. It just never really stops or slows down enough for me to just look at what's right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your work and your school days, do these struggles with focus and staying still cause you significant difficulty in your home life or your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's mostly a problem at work. Like I said, it's really putting my job on the line. Um... I'm supposed to be working toward some certifications, but that's at risk right now. My supervisors have actually written me up a few times because of safety issues and just... not meeting production goals. And it affects how I get along with people there, too. My coworkers are getting frustrated, and they're a lot less willing to partner with me when we have to do handoffs on the floor. So, yeah, it's definitely causing some major issues there.\n\nDoctor: Looking at your personal life outside of work, do you find that you also struggle with being disorganized, losing things, or being forgetful in your daily routine at home?\n\nPatient: I mean, I forget normal stuff all the time. It happens at home, but it's just so much worse at work. Like, I'll forget to clock a step on the checklist, or I'll completely forget to answer a supervisor's text message. Or I'll get all the way out to the floor and realize I forgot to bring my safety glasses from my locker. It just happens constantly, and then it makes it look like I just don't care, but I literally just forget.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the trouble you have at work, do you find that these same issues with being disorganized, forgetful, or unable to sit still frequently cause problems for you in your social life or at home with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Um, not really? I mean, I guess I'm still the same way at home, but it doesn't really cause big problems with my family or my friends. Like, my friends don't really care if I'm moving around a lot or if I forget something. They're pretty used to it, I guess. And at home, I just kind of do my own thing, so nobody's really getting on my case about losing stuff or not sitting still. It's pretty much just at work where it's actually messing things up for me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that these issues don't cause major problems with your friends or family, but have you ever felt that your difficulty staying still or focusing has negatively impacted your ability to maintain friendships or caused conflict within your household?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, I haven't noticed anything like that. Um... I don't really have trouble keeping friends, and we don't get into arguments at home over me moving around or whatever. Like I was saying, it really is just my job where it's causing actual trouble for me.\n\nDoctor: Even if your friends and family aren't upset by your behavior, do you find that your forgetfulness, difficulty staying still, or trouble focusing ever actually prevent you from successfully managing your personal responsibilities, such as paying bills, keeping appointments, or maintaining a clean living space?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't think so? I pay my rent on time and everything. My place isn't, like, perfectly spotless, but it's not a disaster either. I just do what I need to do. Like I said, the only place I'm really dropping the ball and getting in trouble for it is at the plant.\n\nDoctor: When you are at home or in situations where you don't have to be productive, do you still feel an internal sense of restlessness or a constant need to be doing something?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um... it's really obvious at the plant. Like, when I try to stand still by my machine, or if I have to sit through a training session, it feels like there's this... pressure building up inside my body. I just get this intense feeling where I have to move, or pace around, or stretch, or just go do something right then. It's like I just can't stay put.\n\nDoctor: When you are at home, do you ever find yourself struggling to complete tasks or stay on track with your personal chores because you get easily distracted or lose your focus?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I get my chores done fine. It's not like I'm leaving things half-finished all over the house or forgetting what I was doing. When I'm home, I just do whatever needs doing and it's fine. It's just work where I'm having all these problems.\n\nDoctor: Even though you mentioned you don't get in trouble at home, do you find that you have to put in significantly more effort or energy than other people to keep track of your personal tasks and stay on top of your responsibilities at home?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, it doesn't feel like I'm trying extra hard or anything. I just... I know what I have to do around the house and I do it. It doesn't take up a bunch of energy to remember to take out the trash or whatever. It's really just a normal amount of effort, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you are in those moments at home where you say you are getting your chores done or relaxing, do you ever find that you are actually moving around, fidgeting, or needing to change activities more often than other people you know, even if it doesn't cause you any trouble?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Um... I don't think I move around more than anyone else when I'm at home. Once I'm done with whatever I need to do, I just sit down and watch TV or hang out. I don't feel like I'm always fidgeting or jumping from one thing to another there. It really is just at the plant where I get that restless feeling I was talking about.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your childhood and adolescence, were there any settings other than school—such as at home, during extracurricular activities, or while visiting relatives—where you were frequently told that you couldn't sit still or that you weren't paying attention?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly it was just at school. Even back in elementary school, I was always the kid who couldn't stay in my seat. I'd talk over the teacher all the time, and I got a lot of notes sent home because I kept blurting things out or just wandering around the room when I wasn't supposed to. But outside of school, like at home or with family, I don't really remember anyone making a big deal about it. It was pretty much just the teachers getting on my case.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the school environment you mentioned, were there any other settings as a child or teenager, such as at home or during social activities, where you were frequently told that you couldn't sit still or that you weren't paying attention?\n\nPatient: No, like I just said, it was really just at school. I don't remember anyone at home or anywhere else telling me I was hyper or anything. It was just my teachers who were always getting onto me.\n\nDoctor: Since you mentioned your struggles were primarily at school as a child and are primarily at work now, can you think of any other specific settings, like extracurricular clubs, sports teams, or part-time jobs you might have had as a teenager, where these behaviors caused similar problems?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I didn't really do any clubs or sports or anything like that. I've never really felt like I was missing out on stuff I actually wanted to do just because I can't sit still. It's more like... my mind is just going a million miles an hour. It just never really stops or slows down enough for me to just look at what's right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your work and your school days, do these struggles with focus and staying still cause you significant difficulty in your home life or your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's mostly a problem at work. Like I said, it's really putting my job on the line. Um... I'm supposed to be working toward some certifications, but that's at risk right now. My supervisors have actually written me up a few times because of safety issues and just... not meeting production goals. And it affects how I get along with people there, too. My coworkers are getting frustrated, and they're a lot less willing to partner with me when we have to do handoffs on the floor. So, yeah, it's definitely causing some major issues there.\n\nDoctor: Looking at your personal life outside of work, do you find that you also struggle with being disorganized, losing things, or being forgetful in your daily routine at home?\n\nPatient: I mean, I forget normal stuff all the time. It happens at home, but it's just so much worse at work. Like, I'll forget to clock a step on the checklist, or I'll completely forget to answer a supervisor's text message. Or I'll get all the way out to the floor and realize I forgot to bring my safety glasses from my locker. It just happens constantly, and then it makes it look like I just don't care, but I literally just forget.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the trouble you have at work, do you find that these same issues with being disorganized, forgetful, or unable to sit still frequently cause problems for you in your social life or at home with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Um, not really? I mean, I guess I'm still the same way at home, but it doesn't really cause big problems with my family or my friends. Like, my friends don't really care if I'm moving around a lot or if I forget something. They're pretty used to it, I guess. And at home, I just kind of do my own thing, so nobody's really getting on my case about losing stuff or not sitting still. It's pretty much just at work where it's actually messing things up for me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that these issues don't cause major problems with your friends or family, but have you ever felt that your difficulty staying still or focusing has negatively impacted your ability to maintain friendships or caused conflict within your household?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, I haven't noticed anything like that. Um... I don't really have trouble keeping friends, and we don't get into arguments at home over me moving around or whatever. Like I was saying, it really is just my job where it's causing actual trouble for me.\n\nDoctor: Even if your friends and family aren't upset by your behavior, do you find that your forgetfulness, difficulty staying still, or trouble focusing ever actually prevent you from successfully managing your personal responsibilities, such as paying bills, keeping appointments, or maintaining a clean living space?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't think so? I pay my rent on time and everything. My place isn't, like, perfectly spotless, but it's not a disaster either. I just do what I need to do. Like I said, the only place I'm really dropping the ball and getting in trouble for it is at the plant.\n\nDoctor: When you are at home or in situations where you don't have to be productive, do you still feel an internal sense of restlessness or a constant need to be doing something?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um... it's really obvious at the plant. Like, when I try to stand still by my machine, or if I have to sit through a training session, it feels like there's this... pressure building up inside my body. I just get this intense feeling where I have to move, or pace around, or stretch, or just go do something right then. It's like I just can't stay put.\n\nDoctor: When you are at home, do you ever find yourself struggling to complete tasks or stay on track with your personal chores because you get easily distracted or lose your focus?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I get my chores done fine. It's not like I'm leaving things half-finished all over the house or forgetting what I was doing. When I'm home, I just do whatever needs doing and it's fine. It's just work where I'm having all these problems.\n\nDoctor: Even though you mentioned you don't get in trouble at home, do you find that you have to put in significantly more effort or energy than other people to keep track of your personal tasks and stay on top of your responsibilities at home?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, it doesn't feel like I'm trying extra hard or anything. I just... I know what I have to do around the house and I do it. It doesn't take up a bunch of energy to remember to take out the trash or whatever. It's really just a normal amount of effort, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you are in those moments at home where you say you are getting your chores done or relaxing, do you ever find that you are actually moving around, fidgeting, or needing to change activities more often than other people you know, even if it doesn't cause you any trouble?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Um... I don't think I move around more than anyone else when I'm at home. Once I'm done with whatever I need to do, I just sit down and watch TV or hang out. I don't feel like I'm always fidgeting or jumping from one thing to another there. It really is just at the plant where I get that restless feeling I was talking about.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your childhood and adolescence, were there any settings other than school—such as at home, during extracurricular activities, or while visiting relatives—where you were frequently told that you couldn't sit still or that you weren't paying attention?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly it was just at school. Even back in elementary school, I was always the kid who couldn't stay in my seat. I'd talk over the teacher all the time, and I got a lot of notes sent home because I kept blurting things out or just wandering around the room when I wasn't supposed to. But outside of school, like at home or with family, I don't really remember anyone making a big deal about it. It was pretty much just the teachers getting on my case.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the school environment you mentioned, were there any other settings as a child or teenager, such as at home or during social activities, where you were frequently told that you couldn't sit still or that you weren't paying attention?\n\nPatient: No, like I just said, it was really just at school. I don't remember anyone at home or anywhere else telling me I was hyper or anything. It was just my teachers who were always getting onto me.\n\nDoctor: Since you mentioned your struggles were primarily at school as a child and are primarily at work now, can you think of any other specific settings, like extracurricular clubs, sports teams, or part-time jobs you might have had as a teenager, where these behaviors caused similar problems?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I didn't really do any clubs or sports or anything like that. I did have a couple of fast-food jobs before I got hired at the plant, but you're always on your feet and moving fast in those places anyway, so nobody ever complained about me being restless or anything. So, yeah, it really was just school.\n\nDoctor: Even though you don't get in trouble at home, do you feel that your difficulty focusing or staying still has ever caused you to miss out on things you wanted to do, or made you feel like you were falling behind on your personal life goals, even if no one else was complaining about it?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I don't really have a bunch of big life goals that I'm falling behind on or anything like that. I just go to work at the plant and then come home. I've never really felt like I was missing out on stuff I actually wanted to do just because I can't sit still. I just do my own thing on my own time, so it hasn't really been an issue for me outside of work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been evaluated by a doctor or mental health professional for these concerns or received any formal diagnosis like ADHD in the past?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. Um, I never went to a doctor for any of this before. Even when my teachers were complaining back in school, nobody ever sent me to get checked out or anything. So, no, I've never been evaluated or had a formal diagnosis. This is my first time.\n\nDoctor: When you are engaged in a hobby or a leisure activity that you find particularly interesting or engaging, do you find that you can sustain your focus and sit still for long periods without any difficulty?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess it depends. Even if I'm doing something I like, I still can't really sit still for very long. Like, I might be into it, but I'll still be shifting around or getting up to grab something or just feeling kind of antsy. I don't really have any hobbies where I just sit completely still for hours, if that's what you mean. I'm pretty much always moving at least a little bit.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
